English for Work · Health & life sciences

Obstetrics English

Obstetric English for pregnancy histories, prenatal screening, urgent maternal concerns, gestational diabetes discussions, birth preferences, labor handoffs, postpartum care, and pregnancy loss.

8 practical lessonsIntermediate to advanced1 complete learner book
Start practicing →

Words into action

Read the situation.
Find your words.
Make yourself clear.

For obstetricians, obstetrics residents, maternal-fetal medicine physicians, maternity-care physicians.

Open learner book (PDF) ↗

Copy complete AI prompts ↓

What you will practice · Choose your pace

What you will practice

  • Clarify dating information and previous pregnancy experiences without making assumptions.
  • Explain screening uncertainty and voluntary next steps without directing a reproductive decision.
  • Communicate urgent symptoms and pregnancy context without delaying the actual response.
  • Explain testing stages and respond to self-blame without assigning a diagnosis or diet plan.
  • Explain a changed clinical recommendation while preserving the patient as an active decision-maker.

Choose your pace

Quick practice · 15 minutes: complete one short exchange, check the explanations, and read both roles aloud.

Extended practice · two or three sittings: check the case facts, study the vocabulary and phrases, complete the longer conversation, then rehearse the follow-up exchange.

Level guide: designed for intermediate to advanced learners. B1 learners can start with the short exchanges and supplied phrases; B2 learners can complete the cloze conversations; C1 learners can focus on the distinctions in the language checks. These are teaching suggestions, not a certified level assessment.

Prompts

All people, records, figures, and encounters are fictional. This is professional English practice, not medical advice, clinical training, a treatment protocol, or certification. Use current evidence, authorized supervision where required, local law, and your actual clinical and emergency procedures. Do not use an exercise to decide that symptoms can safely wait, select treatment, or calculate a dose. Communication about privacy, consent, and professional duties depends on jurisdiction and setting. Use qualified language support and protect real patient information.

01Taking a sensitive pregnancy historyDates, previous experiences, and preferred words+

Your goal

Clarify dating information and previous pregnancy experiences without making assumptions.

This lesson in the book, page 4 ↗

A Read the situation

Dates, previous experiences, and preferred words

At an initial visit, Avery is unsure of the last menstrual period and mentions a previous pregnancy loss. Dr Stone asks permission to discuss the history, records uncertain dates as uncertain, and asks which support person Avery wants involved. No estimated due date, gestational age, or pregnancy outcome is calculated in the case.

Avery: Pregnant patient / Dr Stone: Obstetrician

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which date is uncertain?

Answer and explanation

A. The last menstrual period

Avery is unsure of the last menstrual period in the supplied history.

2. What previous experience is mentioned?

Answer and explanation

A. Pregnancy loss

Avery mentions a previous loss without supplying a detailed obstetric history.

3. What is not calculated?

Answer and explanation

C. Gestational age or due date

The case supplies no data or instruction for calculating gestational age or a due date.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

An uncertain menstrual date must not become a precise due date by assumption. A history abbreviation does not replace clarification of what actually occurred.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
gravidity
Number of pregnancies, counted under the relevant clinical convention.document gravidity
parity
Birth history summarized using the applicable clinical convention.clarify parity
gestational age
Duration of pregnancy expressed using the relevant dating method.explain gestational age
estimated due date
Estimated date used for pregnancy planning and dating.verify the estimated due date
last menstrual period
Reported starting date of the most recent menstrual period.clarify the last menstrual period
dating ultrasound
Ultrasound used to inform pregnancy dating in context.discuss a dating ultrasound
menstrual regularity
Pattern of how consistently menstrual cycles occur.ask about menstrual regularity
obstetric history
Account of previous pregnancies and their relevant outcomes.obtain an obstetric history
prior pregnancy loss
Loss in an earlier pregnancy.acknowledge a prior pregnancy loss
live birth
Birth showing signs of life under the relevant definition.document a live birth
preterm birth
Birth before the relevant full-term threshold.review a preterm-birth history
ectopic pregnancy
Pregnancy implanted outside the usual uterine cavity location.clarify an ectopic-pregnancy history
prenatal record
Record of pregnancy-related care before birth.review the prenatal record
support person
Person the patient chooses to provide support.identify the chosen support person
preferred terminology
Words the person wishes used for sensitive experiences.ask about preferred terminology
trauma-informed approach
Approach attentive to safety, choice, and prior distressing experiences.use a trauma-informed approach
dating uncertainty
Unresolved information affecting pregnancy dating.document dating uncertainty
history clarification
Follow-up questions resolving ambiguous historical information.request history clarification
qualified interpreter
Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers.request a qualified interpreter
teach-back
Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning.use teach-back without blame
patient preference
A person's stated wishes about care or communication.elicit the patient preference
clinical handoff
Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals.confirm the clinical handoff
documented plan
Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved.review the documented plan
source attribution
Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.preserve source attribution

Counting conventions and terminology differ. Confirm the actual record and local convention; do not calculate a pregnancy outcome or dating estimate from this exercise.

C Notice the language

Use the person's words

A previous loss may carry a meaning not captured by a count. Ask respectfully, avoid assumptions about family structure, and separate necessary history from curiosity.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Ask permission
May we discuss your previous pregnancy experiences?
Allow uncertainty
It is fine to say that you do not remember the exact date.
Keep the record honest
I will record the date as uncertain.
Ask about words
Is there a term you would prefer us to use?
Include support by choice
Who would you like involved in this conversation?
Explain relevance
I will explain why I am asking each sensitive question.
Avoid assumptions
I will not infer the outcome from an abbreviation alone.
Summarize respectfully
Let me check that I have understood your history correctly.
Invite correction
Please stop me if I have misunderstood.
Explain a term
In everyday language, that means the following.
Separate uncertainty
That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding.
Check your explanation
Let me explain that another way.
Make space
We can pause before moving to the next point.
Name responsibility
Let us confirm who will follow up.
Keep a record accurate
I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed.
Confirm an agreement
We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
6 language and precision notes
Estimated versus guaranteed
An estimated due date is a planning estimate, not a promise of the birth date.
Unsure versus approximate
Unsure identifies uncertainty; an approximate date still requires a basis in the person's account.
Chosen support person
Identifies the patient's preference without assigning decision authority to a companion.
Reported versus established
Attribute a person's account with reports or describes. Use confirms only when the relevant evidence supports confirmation.
Can versus will
Can describes an available possibility or ability; will makes a commitment. Do not turn a possible result into a promise.
Checking a message
A check-back confirms the meaning received. Correct the explanation calmly when the listener has understood something different.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which entry preserves the history?

Answer and explanation

C. Last menstrual period uncertain.

The entry accurately retains uncertainty rather than manufacturing precision.

2. Which opening is respectful?

Answer and explanation

B. May we discuss your previous pregnancy experiences?

Asking permission creates space for a sensitive history without assuming the person's experience.

3. What should an abbreviation not replace?

Answer and explanation

D. Clarification of the actual history

Clinical shorthand may use different conventions and cannot replace the relevant facts.

4. Which role is not automatic for a companion?

Answer and explanation

D. Authorized decision-maker

Accompanying a patient does not by itself establish decision-making or information-sharing authority.

Check explanations in the book, page 85 ↗

E Conversations

Dates, previous experiences, and preferred words

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Avery: Pregnant patient / Dr Stone: Obstetrician

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • trauma-informed approach
  • Parity
  • history clarification
  • estimated due date
  • preferred terminology
  • Gravidity
  • Dating uncertainty
  • last menstrual period
  • support person
  • obstetric history
  1. Dr Stone
    Before we discuss dates, may I ask about previous pregnancy experiences and how you would like us to refer to them?
  2. Avery
    Yes. I had a pregnancy loss, and I would prefer that wording rather than moving straight to a number.
  3. Dr Stone
    I will respect that 1. The history matters, but the experience is more than an abbreviation.
  4. Avery
    I am also unsure of the exact date of my last period. I do not want to give a false answer.
  5. Dr Stone
    We can record the 2 as uncertain rather than turn a guess into a precise date.
  6. Avery
    Does that mean the date on my calendar is not automatically the date we should use for planning?
  7. Dr Stone
    Correct. 3 needs to remain visible while we review the relevant information.
  8. Avery
    A previous form used letters and numbers that I did not understand. Could those be checked with me?
  9. Dr Stone
    Yes. Your 4 should reflect what actually happened, not an assumption based only on shorthand.
  10. Avery
    One word was gravidity. Does that refer to every pregnancy rather than just births?
  11. Dr Stone
    5 concerns the number of pregnancies under the relevant convention; we will clarify the details respectfully.
  12. Avery
    And parity is a different summary, so I should not assume the two numbers mean the same thing?
  13. Dr Stone
    Yes. 6 uses the applicable birth-history convention. The underlying facts still need to be clear.
  14. Avery
    I would like my partner involved later, but I want this first part of the conversation to remain between us.
  15. Dr Stone
    We can discuss the appropriate privacy arrangements and when your chosen 7 joins the conversation.
  16. Avery
    Will a scan necessarily give a guaranteed day on which the baby will be born?
  17. Dr Stone
    No. An 8 is an estimate, and its basis should be explained.
  18. Avery
    I appreciate knowing what is uncertain instead of feeling that I must remember every detail perfectly.
  19. Dr Stone
    A 9 includes explaining the purpose of questions and allowing appropriate choice and pauses.
  20. Avery
    Then we can complete the 10 with uncertain dates marked honestly and the previous loss described in my words.

Answers and explanations
  1. preferred terminology Turn 3. Preferred terminology records the person's chosen words for a sensitive experience rather than imposing a label without checking its meaning.
  2. last menstrual period Turn 5. The last menstrual period is a reported dating detail and should remain uncertain when the patient cannot supply a reliable date.
  3. Dating uncertainty Turn 7. Dating uncertainty identifies unresolved information affecting pregnancy dating and should remain visible while the appropriate assessment is arranged.
  4. obstetric history Turn 9. Obstetric history describes prior pregnancies and relevant outcomes and should be clarified rather than inferred solely from unfamiliar shorthand.
  5. Gravidity Turn 11. Gravidity counts pregnancies under the relevant convention and differs from a summary of birth history.
  6. Parity Turn 13. Parity summarizes birth history using the applicable clinical convention and is not interchangeable with gravidity.
  7. support person Turn 15. A support person is someone the patient chooses for support and is not automatically entitled to every disclosure or decision.
  8. estimated due date Turn 17. An estimated due date is a planning and dating estimate rather than a guarantee of the actual day of birth.
  9. trauma-informed approach Turn 19. A trauma-informed approach attends to safety, choice, and prior distress without forcing unnecessary disclosure or ignoring the person's preferences.
  10. history clarification Turn 20. History clarification resolves ambiguous information through respectful follow-up rather than replacing missing details with assumptions.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Dr Stone: Before we discuss dates, may I ask about previous pregnancy experiences and how you would like us to refer to them?
  2. Avery: Yes. I had a pregnancy loss, and I would prefer that wording rather than moving straight to a number.
  3. Dr Stone: I will respect that preferred terminology. The history matters, but the experience is more than an abbreviation.
  4. Avery: I am also unsure of the exact date of my last period. I do not want to give a false answer.
  5. Dr Stone: We can record the last menstrual period as uncertain rather than turn a guess into a precise date.
  6. Avery: Does that mean the date on my calendar is not automatically the date we should use for planning?
  7. Dr Stone: Correct. Dating uncertainty needs to remain visible while we review the relevant information.
  8. Avery: A previous form used letters and numbers that I did not understand. Could those be checked with me?
  9. Dr Stone: Yes. Your obstetric history should reflect what actually happened, not an assumption based only on shorthand.
  10. Avery: One word was gravidity. Does that refer to every pregnancy rather than just births?
  11. Dr Stone: Gravidity concerns the number of pregnancies under the relevant convention; we will clarify the details respectfully.
  12. Avery: And parity is a different summary, so I should not assume the two numbers mean the same thing?
  13. Dr Stone: Yes. Parity uses the applicable birth-history convention. The underlying facts still need to be clear.
  14. Avery: I would like my partner involved later, but I want this first part of the conversation to remain between us.
  15. Dr Stone: We can discuss the appropriate privacy arrangements and when your chosen support person joins the conversation.
  16. Avery: Will a scan necessarily give a guaranteed day on which the baby will be born?
  17. Dr Stone: No. An estimated due date is an estimate, and its basis should be explained.
  18. Avery: I appreciate knowing what is uncertain instead of feeling that I must remember every detail perfectly.
  19. Dr Stone: A trauma-informed approach includes explaining the purpose of questions and allowing appropriate choice and pauses.
  20. Avery: Then we can complete the history clarification with uncertain dates marked honestly and the previous loss described in my words.
Dialogue in the book, page 10 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

An uncertain date stays uncertain

A patient clarifies pregnancy-dating information.

  1. Patient: I can remember the month of my last period, but not the exact starting date.
  2. Obstetrician: We should record that uncertainty rather than create a precise date from a guess.
  3. Patient: Does an approximate memory automatically become a confirmed gestational age?
  4. Obstetrician: No. The actual dating assessment needs the relevant information and an explained basis.
  5. Patient: I do not want a calendar estimate presented as a guaranteed birth date.
  6. Obstetrician: An estimated due date is a planning estimate, not a promise about the day of birth.
  7. Patient: Could the record distinguish what I reported from what a later assessment establishes?
  8. Obstetrician: Yes. The source and basis of the dating information should remain clear.
  9. Patient: Then I can give the most accurate account I have without feeling forced to invent detail.
  10. Obstetrician: That supports a better assessment than false precision in an apparently complete form.
A previous loss needs respectful wording

A clinician asks permission for a sensitive history.

  1. Obstetrician: May we discuss your previous pregnancy experiences and the words you would prefer us to use?
  2. Patient: I would like you to say pregnancy loss rather than move immediately to an abbreviation.
  3. Obstetrician: I will respect that and explain why the relevant history matters.
  4. Patient: The experience was difficult, and I do not want unnecessary detail requested out of curiosity.
  5. Obstetrician: We should ask what is relevant to care and give you appropriate space to respond.
  6. Patient: Could my chosen support person join after this first part of the discussion?
  7. Obstetrician: Yes. We can clarify your preference and the appropriate information-sharing arrangements.
  8. Patient: I also want the actual history checked rather than inferred from an unfamiliar number.
  9. Obstetrician: The underlying facts matter more than shorthand whose convention may be unclear.
  10. Patient: Then the record can be clinically useful without erasing the meaning of the experience.
A companion is not automatically the decision-maker

A patient clarifies support roles.

  1. Patient: My partner handles appointment messages, but I want the clinical explanation directed to me.
  2. Obstetrician: That is appropriate. Administrative help does not automatically transfer your decision-making role.
  3. Patient: I may want them present for some discussions but not every personal question.
  4. Obstetrician: We should confirm your preferences and the actual privacy arrangements.
  5. Patient: Does speaking more English give them authority to answer sensitive questions for me?
  6. Obstetrician: No. We can arrange qualified language support where needed without replacing your voice.
  7. Patient: I would like the record to distinguish support from permission to receive every detail.
  8. Obstetrician: That distinction should remain clear for the whole team.
  9. Patient: Could we revisit the arrangement if my preferences change later?
  10. Obstetrician: Yes. We should record the actual current preference and follow the relevant consent and privacy process.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Dates, previous experiences, and preferred words
  1. Read the corrected history discussion. Preserve uncertain dates and the preferred terminology.
  2. Swap roles. Explain the difference between history terms without calculating a due date.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A record uses unfamiliar shorthand

An outside record contains an unfamiliar pregnancy-history abbreviation. The patient has not confirmed its meaning. The clinician requests clarification before adopting it.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Patient: "I do not recognize that ___."

Answer and explanation

B. abbreviation

The outside record contains shorthand the patient does not recognize.

2. Clinician: "Its meaning is not yet ___."

Answer and explanation

C. confirmed

The patient has not confirmed what the abbreviation represents.

3. Patient: "We need to clarify the actual ___."

Answer and explanation

C. history

The underlying pregnancy history matters more than assuming the shorthand is accurate.

4. Clinician: "I will not adopt it by ___."

Answer and explanation

A. assumption

The clinician requests clarification rather than treating the unverified entry as established.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Another round with an AI partner

Extend the vocabularyPractice the grammarRehearse with AI

Copy a finished AI prompt for this lesson

These prompts are fully written and include this lesson's facts and language. Open one, copy it as written, and paste it into a new AI chat.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 01: Taking a sensitive pregnancy history
Original fictional case: At an initial visit, Avery is unsure of the last menstrual period and mentions a previous pregnancy loss. Dr Stone asks permission to discuss the history, records uncertain dates as uncertain, and asks which support person Avery wants involved. No estimated due date, gestational age, or pregnancy outcome is calculated in the case.
Language workshop: Dates, previous experiences, and preferred words
Communication goal: Clarify dating information and previous pregnancy experiences without making assumptions.
Language guidance: An uncertain menstrual date must not become a precise due date by assumption. A history abbreviation does not replace clarification of what actually occurred.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): May we discuss your previous pregnancy experiences? / It is fine to say that you do not remember the exact date. / I will record the date as uncertain. / Is there a term you would prefer us to use? / Who would you like involved in this conversation? / I will explain why I am asking each sensitive question. / I will not infer the outcome from an abbreviation alone. / Let me check that I have understood your history correctly. / Please stop me if I have misunderstood. / In everyday language, that means the following. / That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding. / Let me explain that another way. / We can pause before moving to the next point. / Let us confirm who will follow up. / I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed. / We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
Vocabulary: gravidity: Number of pregnancies, counted under the relevant clinical convention. / parity: Birth history summarized using the applicable clinical convention. / gestational age: Duration of pregnancy expressed using the relevant dating method. / estimated due date: Estimated date used for pregnancy planning and dating. / last menstrual period: Reported starting date of the most recent menstrual period. / dating ultrasound: Ultrasound used to inform pregnancy dating in context. / menstrual regularity: Pattern of how consistently menstrual cycles occur. / obstetric history: Account of previous pregnancies and their relevant outcomes. / prior pregnancy loss: Loss in an earlier pregnancy. / live birth: Birth showing signs of life under the relevant definition. / preterm birth: Birth before the relevant full-term threshold. / ectopic pregnancy: Pregnancy implanted outside the usual uterine cavity location. / prenatal record: Record of pregnancy-related care before birth. / support person: Person the patient chooses to provide support. / preferred terminology: Words the person wishes used for sensitive experiences. / trauma-informed approach: Approach attentive to safety, choice, and prior distressing experiences. / dating uncertainty: Unresolved information affecting pregnancy dating. / history clarification: Follow-up questions resolving ambiguous historical information. / qualified interpreter: Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers. / teach-back: Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning. / patient preference: A person's stated wishes about care or communication. / clinical handoff: Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals. / documented plan: Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved. / source attribution: Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.
Speaking task: Read the corrected history discussion. Preserve uncertain dates and the preferred terminology. Swap roles. Explain the difference between history terms without calculating a due date. Complete the four-line follow-up with the supplied words. Check every explanation, then read the corrected exchange twice, changing roles.
Partner: Avery: Pregnant patient / Dr Stone: Obstetrician
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: An outside record contains an unfamiliar pregnancy-history abbreviation. The patient has not confirmed its meaning. The clinician requests clarification before adopting it.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Dr Stone: No. An estimated due date is an estimate, and its basis should be explained.
Avery: I appreciate knowing what is uncertain instead of feeling that I must remember every detail perfectly.
Dr Stone: A trauma-informed approach includes explaining the purpose of questions and allowing appropriate choice and pauses.
Avery: Then we can complete the history clarification with uncertain dates marked honestly and the previous loss described in my words.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Vocabulary: gravidity: Number of pregnancies, counted under the relevant clinical convention. / parity: Birth history summarized using the applicable clinical convention. / gestational age: Duration of pregnancy expressed using the relevant dating method. / estimated due date: Estimated date used for pregnancy planning and dating. / last menstrual period: Reported starting date of the most recent menstrual period. / dating ultrasound: Ultrasound used to inform pregnancy dating in context. / menstrual regularity: Pattern of how consistently menstrual cycles occur. / obstetric history: Account of previous pregnancies and their relevant outcomes. / prior pregnancy loss: Loss in an earlier pregnancy. / live birth: Birth showing signs of life under the relevant definition. / preterm birth: Birth before the relevant full-term threshold. / ectopic pregnancy: Pregnancy implanted outside the usual uterine cavity location. / prenatal record: Record of pregnancy-related care before birth. / support person: Person the patient chooses to provide support. / preferred terminology: Words the person wishes used for sensitive experiences. / trauma-informed approach: Approach attentive to safety, choice, and prior distressing experiences. / dating uncertainty: Unresolved information affecting pregnancy dating. / history clarification: Follow-up questions resolving ambiguous historical information. / qualified interpreter: Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers. / teach-back: Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning. / patient preference: A person's stated wishes about care or communication. / clinical handoff: Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals. / documented plan: Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved. / source attribution: Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.
Speaking task: Read the corrected history discussion. Preserve uncertain dates and the preferred terminology. Swap roles. Explain the difference between history terms without calculating a due date. Dr Stone: A trauma-informed approach includes explaining the purpose of questions and allowing appropriate choice and pauses.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

02Explaining prenatal screening choicesA higher chance is not a diagnosis+

Your goal

Explain screening uncertainty and voluntary next steps without directing a reproductive decision.

This lesson in the book, page 13 ↗

A Read the situation

A higher chance is not a diagnosis

Riley receives a higher-chance prenatal screening result and thinks it proves a fetal condition. Dr Noor explains that screening estimates chance rather than establishing a diagnosis. The actual result and available diagnostic options need individualized counseling. No probability value, procedure choice, or pregnancy decision is supplied. Riley wants time to understand the information and include a chosen support person.

Riley: Pregnant patient / Dr Noor: Obstetrician

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does the result describe?

Answer and explanation

B. A higher estimated chance

The case describes screening probability rather than diagnostic confirmation.

2. What does Riley request?

Answer and explanation

C. Time and a chosen support person

Riley wants to understand the information with appropriate support.

3. What is not supplied?

Answer and explanation

D. A numerical probability or procedure choice

The exercise supplies neither an individual probability value nor a selected diagnostic procedure.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A higher-chance screening result is not diagnostic confirmation. A lower-chance result does not guarantee absence of every condition.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
prenatal screening
Testing that estimates the chance of specified conditions before birth.explain prenatal screening
diagnostic testing
Testing intended to establish or clarify a condition.discuss diagnostic testing
cell-free DNA screening
Screening using DNA fragments in maternal blood for specified conditions.explain cell-free DNA screening
screening probability
Estimated chance reported by a screening process.communicate screening probability
screen-positive result
Screening finding indicating increased chance under the relevant method.explain a screen-positive result
false-positive result
Positive screening finding not confirmed for the condition assessed.explain a false-positive result
false-negative result
Negative screening finding despite the condition being present.explain a false-negative result
positive predictive value
Proportion of positive results that correspond to the condition in context.discuss positive predictive value
residual risk
Remaining chance after a result or intervention.explain residual risk
genetic counseling
Specialist discussion of genetic information, options, and implications.arrange genetic counseling
amniocentesis
Procedure obtaining amniotic fluid for relevant testing.discuss amniocentesis
chorionic villus sampling
Procedure obtaining placental tissue for relevant testing.explain chorionic villus sampling
chromosomal condition
Condition involving chromosome number or structure.discuss a chromosomal condition
test limitation
Boundary on what a test can detect or establish.explain test limitations
inconclusive result
Result that does not provide a definitive answer to its question.communicate an inconclusive result
informed refusal
Decision to decline after relevant information is understood.document informed refusal
voluntary testing
Testing chosen without coercion after appropriate discussion.support voluntary testing
decision support
Assistance helping a person understand options and relevant values.offer decision support
qualified interpreter
Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers.request a qualified interpreter
teach-back
Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning.use teach-back without blame
patient preference
A person's stated wishes about care or communication.elicit the patient preference
clinical handoff
Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals.confirm the clinical handoff
documented plan
Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved.review the documented plan
source attribution
Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.preserve source attribution

Explain actual test performance and options in context. Do not supply an invented probability, universal procedure recommendation, or reproductive decision.

C Notice the language

Explain choices without steering personal values

Give clear information about the purpose and limits of testing. Do not assume what the patient would do with a result or pressure a reproductive decision.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Name the result type
This is a screening result, not a diagnosis.
Explain the limit
It changes the estimated chance but does not settle the question.
Avoid a guarantee
A lower-chance result cannot rule out every condition.
Offer counseling
We can arrange a discussion of the actual result and options.
Ask about information
What would you like explained before considering the next step?
Protect choice
Testing decisions require an informed, voluntary discussion.
Include chosen support
We can include the person you choose with your permission.
Allow a pause
We can pause to make sure the information is clear.
Invite correction
Please stop me if I have misunderstood.
Explain a term
In everyday language, that means the following.
Separate uncertainty
That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding.
Check your explanation
Let me explain that another way.
Make space
We can pause before moving to the next point.
Name responsibility
Let us confirm who will follow up.
Keep a record accurate
I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed.
Confirm an agreement
We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
6 language and precision notes
Chance versus certainty
Keeps a probability estimate distinct from a confirmed finding.
For specified conditions
Prevents one screening method from being described as testing for everything.
May choose versus must
Preserves voluntary decision-making rather than presenting a procedure as automatic.
Reported versus established
Attribute a person's account with reports or describes. Use confirms only when the relevant evidence supports confirmation.
Can versus will
Can describes an available possibility or ability; will makes a commitment. Do not turn a possible result into a promise.
Checking a message
A check-back confirms the meaning received. Correct the explanation calmly when the listener has understood something different.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which explanation is accurate?

Answer and explanation

B. Screening estimates chance; diagnostic testing addresses confirmation.

Screening and diagnosis answer different questions and cannot be treated as interchangeable.

2. Which statement overextends the test?

Answer and explanation

B. This screens for every possible condition.

A screening method has a defined scope and cannot be described as detecting every condition.

3. What does positive predictive value concern?

Answer and explanation

A. How often a positive result corresponds to the condition in context

The measure concerns the meaning of positive results in the relevant population and context.

4. Which next-step statement respects choice?

Answer and explanation

B. Let us discuss the options and your questions.

An informed discussion supports the patient's decision without imposing a reproductive choice.

Check explanations in the book, page 87 ↗

E Conversations

A higher chance is not a diagnosis

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Riley: Pregnant patient / Dr Noor: Obstetrician

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • Genetic counseling
  • screening probability
  • Decision support
  • Amniocentesis
  • false-positive result
  • Residual risk
  • Voluntary testing
  • inconclusive result
  • Positive predictive value
  • prenatal screening
  1. Riley
    The message says higher chance. I read that as proof that the baby has the condition.
  2. Dr Noor
    This is 1, not diagnostic confirmation. Let us explain what this particular result can and cannot establish.
  3. Riley
    Does that mean the result is meaningless, or that I should simply ignore it?
  4. Dr Noor
    No. The 2 informs the next discussion, but an estimate is not certainty.
  5. Riley
    I would like to understand how a positive result can occur when the condition is not present.
  6. Dr Noor
    That is a 3. The actual test and your circumstances determine how the result should be interpreted.
  7. Riley
    And a lower-chance result would not mean every possible condition has been excluded?
  8. Dr Noor
    Correct. 4 and the scope of the test still matter.
  9. Riley
    Someone mentioned a predictive value, but I did not understand what was being predicted.
  10. Dr Noor
    5 concerns what positive results mean in context. We should use the actual figures, not invent a number.
  11. Riley
    What kind of discussion would help me understand the further testing options?
  12. Dr Noor
    6 can help explain the actual result, options, limitations, and what information you want.
  13. Riley
    Are amniocentesis and the placental-sampling procedure simply more screening blood tests?
  14. Dr Noor
    No. 7 and chorionic villus sampling are procedures used for relevant diagnostic testing, with their own discussions of benefits and risks.
  15. Riley
    I do not want a procedure selected before I understand what question it would answer.
  16. Dr Noor
    That is appropriate. 8 should clarify the question and your preferences rather than make the decision for you.
  17. Riley
    Could I include my chosen support person when we discuss the information again?
  18. Dr Noor
    Yes, with your permission. 9 requires a discussion you understand, with support you choose.
  19. Riley
    I also want to know what happens if the further result still does not answer everything.
  20. Dr Noor
    We should explain the possibility of an 10 and other limits before you choose the next step.

Answers and explanations
  1. prenatal screening Turn 2. Prenatal screening estimates chance; it does not itself establish a diagnosis.
  2. screening probability Turn 4. Screening probability informs discussion but is not certainty about an individual outcome.
  3. false-positive result Turn 6. A false-positive result is not confirmed for the condition being assessed.
  4. Residual risk Turn 8. Residual risk remains after screening; lower chance is not a universal guarantee.
  5. Positive predictive value Turn 10. Positive predictive value describes positive results in context, not a personal guarantee.
  6. Genetic counseling Turn 12. Genetic counseling supports understanding of genetic information, testing options, limitations, and implications without imposing a personal reproductive decision.
  7. Amniocentesis Turn 14. Amniocentesis obtains amniotic fluid for relevant testing and is not simply another screening blood draw.
  8. Decision support Turn 16. Decision support helps the patient understand options and relevant values rather than choosing a procedure or pregnancy outcome for them.
  9. Voluntary testing Turn 18. Voluntary testing is chosen without coercion after an appropriate informed discussion, not automatically required by a screening result.
  10. inconclusive result Turn 20. An inconclusive result leaves its relevant question unresolved and should be discussed without promising that every test will provide certainty.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Riley: The message says higher chance. I read that as proof that the baby has the condition.
  2. Dr Noor: This is prenatal screening, not diagnostic confirmation. Let us explain what this particular result can and cannot establish.
  3. Riley: Does that mean the result is meaningless, or that I should simply ignore it?
  4. Dr Noor: No. The screening probability informs the next discussion, but an estimate is not certainty.
  5. Riley: I would like to understand how a positive result can occur when the condition is not present.
  6. Dr Noor: That is a false-positive result. The actual test and your circumstances determine how the result should be interpreted.
  7. Riley: And a lower-chance result would not mean every possible condition has been excluded?
  8. Dr Noor: Correct. Residual risk and the scope of the test still matter.
  9. Riley: Someone mentioned a predictive value, but I did not understand what was being predicted.
  10. Dr Noor: Positive predictive value concerns what positive results mean in context. We should use the actual figures, not invent a number.
  11. Riley: What kind of discussion would help me understand the further testing options?
  12. Dr Noor: Genetic counseling can help explain the actual result, options, limitations, and what information you want.
  13. Riley: Are amniocentesis and the placental-sampling procedure simply more screening blood tests?
  14. Dr Noor: No. Amniocentesis and chorionic villus sampling are procedures used for relevant diagnostic testing, with their own discussions of benefits and risks.
  15. Riley: I do not want a procedure selected before I understand what question it would answer.
  16. Dr Noor: That is appropriate. Decision support should clarify the question and your preferences rather than make the decision for you.
  17. Riley: Could I include my chosen support person when we discuss the information again?
  18. Dr Noor: Yes, with your permission. Voluntary testing requires a discussion you understand, with support you choose.
  19. Riley: I also want to know what happens if the further result still does not answer everything.
  20. Dr Noor: We should explain the possibility of an inconclusive result and other limits before you choose the next step.
Dialogue in the book, page 19 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Higher chance is not confirmation

A patient asks about a prenatal screening message.

  1. Patient: The message says higher chance, and I understood that as a definite diagnosis.
  2. Obstetrician: A screening result estimates chance; it does not establish the diagnosis by itself.
  3. Patient: Does that mean the result should be ignored because it is not certain?
  4. Obstetrician: No. It informs the appropriate counseling and discussion of the actual next options.
  5. Patient: I want to know which condition the test assessed rather than assume it tested for everything.
  6. Obstetrician: The test's scope and limitations belong in the explanation.
  7. Patient: Could we discuss the actual probability without borrowing a number from someone else's result?
  8. Obstetrician: Yes. The relevant figures need the real test and clinical context.
  9. Patient: Then I can understand the result without being pushed toward a reproductive decision.
  10. Obstetrician: That is the aim: clear information, appropriate support, and an informed voluntary discussion.
A negative screen is not a universal guarantee

A clinician explains a screening limitation.

  1. Obstetrician: You asked whether a lower-chance result rules out every possible condition.
  2. Patient: I thought a reassuring screening result meant there was no remaining uncertainty.
  3. Obstetrician: The result has a defined scope, and residual risk may remain.
  4. Patient: Could you explain what this particular test addresses and what it does not?
  5. Obstetrician: We should use the actual test information rather than a general promise.
  6. Patient: I also want to distinguish screening from a diagnostic procedure.
  7. Obstetrician: Those processes answer different questions and need their own discussion.
  8. Patient: A reassuring result should not be described as a guarantee of every future outcome.
  9. Obstetrician: Correct. We can acknowledge useful information while explaining its limits honestly.
  10. Patient: Then I can understand the reassurance without giving it a meaning the test cannot support.
Information gathering is not consent to testing

A patient asks about diagnostic options.

  1. Patient: I want to hear about diagnostic testing, but I have not decided to have a procedure.
  2. Obstetrician: Asking for information does not itself establish consent.
  3. Patient: Please explain the actual options, what they can answer, and their relevant benefits and risks.
  4. Obstetrician: That discussion should also include limitations and the alternatives available in your situation.
  5. Patient: I would like my chosen support person involved after I understand the main points.
  6. Obstetrician: We can arrange that with your permission and appropriate language support if needed.
  7. Patient: Does a specialist referral mean the procedure has already been booked?
  8. Obstetrician: No. Referral, consultation, decision, and appointment confirmation are separate steps.
  9. Patient: I appreciate keeping those stages distinct instead of treating questions as agreement.
  10. Obstetrician: We will record the decision actually made, not one inferred from your interest in the information.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A higher chance is not a diagnosis
  1. Read the corrected screening exchange, emphasizing chance, confirmation, and limits.
  2. Swap roles. Explain the options discussion without assigning a probability or choosing a procedure.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A result is inconclusive

A prenatal test result is described as inconclusive. The clinician arranges review of the actual report and options. No diagnosis is established.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Patient: "The result is ___."

Answer and explanation

B. inconclusive

The result does not provide a definitive answer to its relevant question.

2. Clinician: "We need to review the actual ___."

Answer and explanation

D. report

The actual report is the basis for explaining the unresolved result.

3. Patient: "A diagnosis is not ___."

Answer and explanation

A. established

An inconclusive result does not establish a diagnosis in this case.

4. Clinician: "We will explain the available ___."

Answer and explanation

C. options

The next step is an informed review of options rather than an automatic decision.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

finding despite the condition being present. / positive predictive value: Proportion of positive results that correspond to the condition in context. / residual risk: Remaining chance after a result or intervention. / genetic counseling: Specialist discussion of genetic information, options, and implications. / amniocentesis: Procedure obtaining amniotic fluid for relevant testing. / chorionic villus sampling: Procedure obtaining placental tissue for relevant testing. / chromosomal condition: Condition involving chromosome number or structure. / test limitation: Boundary on what a test can detect or establish. / inconclusive result: Result that does not provide a definitive answer to its question. / informed refusal: Decision to decline after relevant information is understood. / voluntary testing: Testing chosen without coercion after appropriate discussion. / decision support: Assistance helping a person understand options and relevant values. / qualified interpreter: Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers. / teach-back: Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning. / patient preference: A person's stated wishes about care or communication. / clinical handoff: Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals. / documented plan: Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved. / source attribution: Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.
Speaking task: Read the corrected screening exchange, emphasizing chance, confirmation, and limits. Swap roles. Explain the options discussion without assigning a probability or choosing a procedure. Complete the four-line follow-up with the supplied words. Check every explanation, then read the corrected exchange twice, changing roles.

Dr Noor: Yes, with your permission. Voluntary testing requires a discussion you understand, with support you choose.
Riley: I also want to know what happens if the further result still does not answer everything.
Dr Noor: We should explain the possibility of an inconclusive result and other limits before you choose the next step.

Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Obstetrics English
Professional audience: obstetricians, obstetrics residents, maternal-fetal medicine physicians, maternity-care physicians
Scope: All people, records, figures, and encounters are fictional. This is professional English practice, not medical advice, clinical training, a treatment protocol, or certification. Use current evidence, authorized supervision where required, local law, and your actual clinical and emergency procedures. Do not use an exercise to decide that symptoms can safely wait, select treatment, or calculate a dose. Communication about privacy, consent, and professional duties depends on jurisdiction and setting. Use qualified language support and protect real patient information.
Lesson 02: Explaining prenatal screening choices
Original fictional case: Riley receives a higher-chance prenatal screening result and thinks it proves a fetal condition. Dr Noor explains that screening estimates chance rather than establishing a diagnosis. The actual result and available diagnostic options need individualized counseling. No probability value, procedure choice, or pregnancy decision is supplied. Riley wants time to understand the information and include a chosen support person.
Language workshop: A higher chance is not a diagnosis
Communication goal: Explain screening uncertainty and voluntary next steps without directing a reproductive decision.
Language guidance: A higher-chance screening result is not diagnostic confirmation. A lower-chance result does not guarantee absence of every condition.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): This is a screening result, not a diagnosis. / It changes the estimated chance but does not settle the question. / A lower-chance result cannot rule out every condition. / We can arrange a discussion of the actual result and options. / What would you like explained before considering the next step? / Testing decisions require an informed, voluntary discussion. / We can include the person you choose with your permission. / We can pause to make sure the information is clear. / Please stop me if I have misunderstood. / In everyday language, that means the following. / That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding. / Let me explain that another way. / We can pause before moving to the next point. / Let us confirm who will follow up. / I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed. / We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.

screening: Screening using DNA fragments in maternal blood for specified conditions. / screening probability: Estimated chance reported by a screening process. / screen-positive result: Screening finding indicating increased chance under the relevant method. / false-positive result: Positive screening finding not confirmed for the condition assessed. / false-negative result: Negative screening finding despite the condition being present. / positive predictive value: Proportion of positive results that correspond to the condition in context. / residual risk: Remaining chance after a result or intervention. / genetic counseling: Specialist discussion of genetic information, options, and implications. / amniocentesis: Procedure obtaining amniotic fluid for relevant testing. / chorionic Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.
