involved. / source attribution: Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.
Speaking task: Read the corrected exchange, distinguishing a recommendation, understanding, and consent. Swap roles. Acknowledge the preference without selecting a procedure or inventing urgency. Complete the four-line follow-up with the supplied words. Check every explanation, then read the corrected exchange twice, changing roles.
Partner: Quinn: Patient in maternity care / Dr Lane: Obstetrician
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A patient clarifies that a nod meant the explanation was heard, not that consent was given. The clinician acknowledges the distinction and continues the actual consent process.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Quinn: Could you summarize the recommendation again in shorter sentences before asking what I understand?
Dr Lane: Yes. That is part of a useful consent discussion, not a delay caused by asking an unreasonable question.
Quinn: Then my original preference is acknowledged, the changed situation is explained, and I can understand the actual options.
Dr Lane: That supports shared decision-making while respecting the real urgency and keeping you involved in the care discussion.
END REFERENCE
06Giving a labor-ward handoffSeparate the patient report from the monitor interpretation+

Your goal

Transfer maternal and fetal information with sources, timing, outstanding tasks, and accepted responsibility.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

Separate the patient report from the monitor interpretation

Dr Chen hands over patient Samira to Dr Ellis on the labor ward. Samira reports stronger contractions and asks that explanations precede examinations. The latest maternal observations and fetal-monitoring interpretation must be verified from the current clinical record; no values or trace classification are supplied here. The clinicians confirm outstanding tasks and responsibility without inventing findings.

Dr Chen: Outgoing obstetrician / Dr Ellis: Receiving obstetrician

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does Samira report?

Answer and explanation

D. Stronger contractions

The case supplies the patient's report of stronger contractions only.

2. What preference should be handed over?

Answer and explanation

C. Explain before examinations

Samira asks for explanations before examinations, which remains relevant to the next team.

3. What requires verification?

Answer and explanation

C. Current observations and monitoring interpretation

The actual clinical values and interpretation must come from the current record.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Patient-reported contraction strength is not a fetal-monitoring interpretation. Use verified sources and timestamps for clinical findings.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
labor progress
Change in the clinical course of labor over time.describe labor progress
contraction pattern
Frequency, duration, and character of uterine contractions in context.report the contraction pattern
cervical dilation
Opening of the cervix assessed during the relevant examination.document cervical dilation
cervical effacement
Thinning of the cervix in the relevant clinical assessment.describe cervical effacement
fetal station
Position of the presenting part relative to pelvic landmarks.report fetal station
membrane status
Whether membranes are intact or ruptured as assessed.clarify membrane status
rupture time
Reported or established time of membrane rupture.verify rupture time
presenting part
Fetal part positioned toward the birth canal.identify the presenting part
fetal heart-rate trace
Recorded fetal heart-rate information for clinical interpretation.review the fetal heart-rate trace
baseline rate
Reference rate assessed over the relevant monitoring interval.describe the baseline rate
variability
Fluctuation in fetal heart rate assessed within the monitoring context.assess variability
deceleration
Decrease in fetal heart rate characterized in clinical context.describe a deceleration
uterine activity
Pattern of uterine contractions or related activity.assess uterine activity
maternal observations
Verified clinical measurements or findings concerning the patient.relay maternal observations
working assessment
Current clinical interpretation subject to relevant review.state the working assessment
outstanding task
Required action not yet completed.identify outstanding tasks
handoff acceptance
Explicit acknowledgment of receiving information and responsibility.confirm handoff acceptance
contingency plan
Agreed response to specified possible changes under the actual care plan.clarify the contingency plan
qualified interpreter
Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers.request a qualified interpreter
teach-back
Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning.use teach-back without blame
patient preference
A person's stated wishes about care or communication.elicit the patient preference
clinical handoff
Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals.confirm the clinical handoff
documented plan
Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved.review the documented plan
source attribution
Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.preserve source attribution

The terminology bank is not a tracing algorithm or examination guide. No values, classification, or treatment response may be invented from this exercise.

C Notice the language

An efficient handoff still includes the patient

Include clinically relevant preferences as well as observations. Do not let a short report erase consent needs, source attribution, or unfinished work.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Lead with context
I am handing over Samira on the labor ward.
Attribute the report
Samira reports stronger contractions.
Preserve the preference
She asks for an explanation before each examination.
Verify the record
Let us check the current observations and their times.
Separate interpretation
The monitoring interpretation needs the actual reviewed trace.
Name unfinished work
This task remains outstanding.
Confirm responsibility
Please confirm who is taking responsibility for that step.
Close the transfer
I acknowledge the handoff and the unresolved items.
Invite correction
Please stop me if I have misunderstood.
Explain a term
In everyday language, that means the following.
Separate uncertainty
That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding.
Check your explanation
Let me explain that another way.
Make space
We can pause before moving to the next point.
Name responsibility
Let us confirm who will follow up.
Keep a record accurate
I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed.
Confirm an agreement
We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
6 language and precision notes
Reports versus assessed
Separates the patient's experience from a professional measurement or interpretation.
Current and timed
Prevents an earlier finding from being repeated as though it were current.
Accepted versus sent
Makes responsibility explicit rather than treating a transmitted report as a completed handoff.
Reported versus established
Attribute a person's account with reports or describes. Use confirms only when the relevant evidence supports confirmation.
Can versus will
Can describes an available possibility or ability; will makes a commitment. Do not turn a possible result into a promise.
Checking a message
A check-back confirms the meaning received. Correct the explanation calmly when the listener has understood something different.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which statement is source-aware?

Answer and explanation

C. Samira reports stronger contractions.

The statement accurately attributes the supplied information to the patient.

2. What does handoff acceptance add?

Answer and explanation

D. Confirmation of received information and responsibility

Acceptance closes the communication loop rather than merely recording that a message was sent.

3. Which finding cannot be invented?

Answer and explanation

A. A fetal trace classification

No trace or classification is supplied in the case.

4. Why include the examination preference?

Answer and explanation

B. It remains relevant to communication and consent.

The preference guides the next team's explanation and does not disappear during handoff.

E Conversations

Separate the patient report from the monitor interpretation

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Dr Chen: Outgoing obstetrician / Dr Ellis: Receiving obstetrician

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • Membrane status
  • contingency plan

  • working assessment

  • maternal observations
  • Variability
  • handoff acceptance
  • outstanding task
  1. Dr Chen
    I am handing over Samira. She reports stronger contractions and asks for explanations before examinations; please retain that preference.
  2. Dr Ellis
    I acknowledge the report. Let us distinguish the patient account from the current verified observations and clinical interpretation.
  3. Dr Chen
    The reported change in the 1 is her account, not a substitute for the actual assessment.
  4. Dr Ellis
    Understood. We should verify current findings and their times rather than copy an earlier summary as current.
  5. Dr Chen
    Let us review the latest 2 in the current record together, including when each was taken.
  6. Dr Ellis
    Please also identify which assessment belongs to which time, particularly where the situation has changed.
  7. Dr Chen
    Any 3 finding needs its actual examination source and time, not an inference from stronger contractions.
  8. Dr Ellis
    The same applies to membrane information: reported history and established findings should not be blended.
  9. Dr Chen
    Yes. 4 and any rupture time require accurate attribution.
  10. Dr Ellis
    What about the fetal monitoring? I need the actual reviewed interpretation rather than a reassuring shorthand label.
  11. Dr Chen
    We need the current 5 and the authorized interpretation. No classification should be guessed from this conversation.
  12. Dr Ellis
    Terms such as variability have specific clinical meaning, so a language definition is not an interpretation rule.
  13. Dr Chen
    Correct. 6 must be assessed in the real context, not assigned because the word appears in a handoff.
  14. Dr Ellis
    Let us identify which tasks are complete and which person is responsible for anything unfinished.
  15. Dr Chen
    Each 7 should remain visible, including the responsible clinician and relevant communication route.
  16. Dr Ellis
    I will confirm responsibility rather than simply say that the message has arrived.
  17. Dr Chen
    That gives us 8. Samira's request for explanation remains part of the plan.
  18. Dr Ellis
    We also need any actual contingency arrangements explained, not an improvised response to a trace we have not reviewed.
  19. Dr Chen
    Agreed. The 9 must come from the current clinical assessment and agreed care.
  20. Dr Ellis
    I accept the handoff, with the 10, sources, unresolved items, and patient preference clearly distinguished.

Answers and explanations
  1. contraction pattern Turn 3. Contraction pattern needs source attribution; patient report is not a measured finding.
  2. maternal observations Turn 5. Maternal observations require actual clinical findings and the relevant assessment time.
  3. cervical dilation Turn 7. Cervical dilation cannot be inferred from a report of stronger contractions.
  4. Membrane status Turn 9. Membrane status needs its actual source and current assessment context.
  5. fetal heart-rate trace Turn 11. A fetal heart-rate trace requires clinical interpretation, not inference from symptoms.
  6. Variability Turn 13. Variability describes fluctuations in fetal heart rate within the monitoring context and its definition does not provide a clinical interpretation algorithm.
  7. outstanding task Turn 15. An outstanding task is an action not yet completed and needs an explicit owner rather than an assumption that the next team knows.
  8. handoff acceptance Turn 17. Handoff acceptance confirms receipt of the information and responsibility, making the transfer more than an unacknowledged message.
  9. contingency plan Turn 19. A contingency plan specifies responses under the actual care plan and cannot be invented from an incomplete language scenario.
  10. working assessment Turn 20. A working assessment is the current clinical interpretation subject to review and must remain separate from unverified values or assumed conclusions.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Dr Chen: I am handing over Samira. She reports stronger contractions and asks for explanations before examinations; please retain that preference.
  2. Dr Ellis: I acknowledge the report. Let us distinguish the patient account from the current verified observations and clinical interpretation.
  3. Dr Chen: The reported change in the contraction pattern is her account, not a substitute for the actual assessment.
  4. Dr Ellis: Understood. We should verify current findings and their times rather than copy an earlier summary as current.
  5. Dr Chen: Let us review the latest maternal observations in the current record together, including when each was taken.
  6. Dr Ellis: Please also identify which assessment belongs to which time, particularly where the situation has changed.
  7. Dr Chen: Any cervical dilation finding needs its actual examination source and time, not an inference from stronger contractions.
  8. Dr Ellis: The same applies to membrane information: reported history and established findings should not be blended.
  9. Dr Chen: Yes. Membrane status and any rupture time require accurate attribution.
  10. Dr Ellis: What about the fetal monitoring? I need the actual reviewed interpretation rather than a reassuring shorthand label.
  11. Dr Chen: We need the current fetal heart-rate trace and the authorized interpretation. No classification should be guessed from this conversation.
  12. Dr Ellis: Terms such as variability have specific clinical meaning, so a language definition is not an interpretation rule.
  13. Dr Chen: Correct. Variability must be assessed in the real context, not assigned because the word appears in a handoff.
  14. Dr Ellis: Let us identify which tasks are complete and which person is responsible for anything unfinished.
  15. Dr Chen: Each outstanding task should remain visible, including the responsible clinician and relevant communication route.
  16. Dr Ellis: I will confirm responsibility rather than simply say that the message has arrived.
  17. Dr Chen: That gives us handoff acceptance. Samira's request for explanation remains part of the plan.
  18. Dr Ellis: We also need any actual contingency arrangements explained, not an improvised response to a trace we have not reviewed.
  19. Dr Chen: Agreed. The contingency plan must come from the current clinical assessment and agreed care.
  20. Dr Ellis: I accept the handoff, with the working assessment, sources, unresolved items, and patient preference clearly distinguished.
Dialogue in the book, page 55 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Reported contractions and assessed findings differ

Two clinicians clarify a labor handoff.

  1. Outgoing clinician: The patient reports stronger contractions, but I have not supplied a new examination finding.
  2. Receiving clinician: Please retain that distinction rather than infer cervical change from the symptom report.
  3. Outgoing clinician: The current clinical record needs to be checked for verified observations and times.
  4. Receiving clinician: Agreed. An earlier value should not be copied forward as though it were current.
  5. Outgoing clinician: The patient also asks for explanations before examinations.
  6. Receiving clinician: That preference belongs in the handoff and should not disappear behind the clinical shorthand.
  7. Outgoing clinician: I will identify the outstanding tasks and the actual responsible clinician for each.
  8. Receiving clinician: Please confirm which responsibilities I am accepting rather than assume a sent report completes transfer.
  9. Outgoing clinician: The handoff will distinguish patient account, assessed findings, and unfinished work.
  10. Receiving clinician: That gives the next team a clear starting point without invented measurements.
A tracing term is not an interpretation algorithm

Two clinicians discuss monitoring language.

  1. Clinician: The handoff uses variability, but the actual tracing and interpretation are not included.
  2. Obstetrician: We need the reviewed clinical information rather than infer a category from the word alone.
  3. Clinician: A glossary definition does not tell us how this particular trace should be classified.
  4. Obstetrician: Correct. The actual monitoring context and authorized assessment are essential.
  5. Clinician: Should the summary say reassuring simply because no abnormal category was supplied?
  6. Obstetrician: No. Missing interpretation is not a normal finding.
  7. Clinician: I will request the current source and preserve the limitation in the handoff.
  8. Obstetrician: Please also check the timestamp so an earlier assessment is not presented as current.
  9. Clinician: Then the language can be technically accurate without pretending the clinical interpretation is complete.
  10. Obstetrician: Exactly. Clear terminology supports assessment; it does not replace it.
An outstanding task needs an owner

A receiving clinician checks an incomplete handoff item.

  1. Receiving clinician: The summary says follow up, but it does not identify who is responsible or what remains unfinished.
  2. Outgoing clinician: The task has not yet been completed, so we need to make that explicit.
  3. Receiving clinician: Please separate a planned action from one that has already occurred.
  4. Outgoing clinician: I will state the actual status and confirm the responsible person through our process.
  5. Receiving clinician: Does the receiving team have the relevant information needed to carry it out?
  6. Outgoing clinician: We need to verify that rather than assume the attachment contains everything.
  7. Receiving clinician: I will acknowledge the responsibility once the actual transfer arrangements are clear.
  8. Outgoing clinician: Thank you. A sent message should not be mistaken for accepted ownership.
  9. Receiving clinician: We also need the appropriate contingency arrangements from the actual care plan.
  10. Outgoing clinician: Agreed. Those must be verified, not improvised from an incomplete language summary.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Separate the patient report from the monitor interpretation
  1. Read the corrected handoff. Attribute the patient report separately from verified clinical findings.
  2. Swap roles. Confirm responsibility without inventing a tracing category or examination value.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

An earlier observation was copied forward

A handoff repeats an observation from 10:00 as current at 12:00. The receiving clinician identifies the timestamp error and requests the actual current record.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Receiver: "That observation was recorded at ___."

Answer and explanation

D. 10:00

The supplied observation belongs to the earlier 10:00 assessment.

2. Sender: "The handoff is occurring at ___."

Answer and explanation

A. 12:00

The current handoff time is 12:00 in the scenario.

3. Receiver: "We need the actual ___ record."

Answer and explanation

B. current

The earlier value must not be presented as a current finding.

4. Sender: "I will correct the ___."

Answer and explanation

B. timestamp

The immediate communication error concerns the observation's time attribution.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Obstetrics English
Professional audience: obstetricians, obstetrics residents, maternal-fetal medicine physicians, maternity-care physicians
Scope: All people, records, figures, and encounters are fictional. This is professional English practice, not medical advice, clinical training, a treatment protocol, or certification. Use current evidence, authorized supervision where required, local law, and your actual clinical and emergency procedures. Do not use an exercise to decide that symptoms can safely wait, select treatment, or calculate a dose. Communication about privacy, consent, and professional duties depends on jurisdiction and setting. Use qualified language support and protect real patient information.
Lesson 06: Giving a labor-ward handoff
Original fictional case: Dr Chen hands over patient Samira to Dr Ellis on the labor ward. Samira reports stronger contractions and asks that explanations precede examinations. The latest maternal observations and fetal-monitoring interpretation must be verified from the current clinical record; no values or trace classification are supplied here. The clinicians confirm outstanding tasks and responsibility without inventing findings.
Language workshop: Separate the patient report from the monitor interpretation
Communication goal: Transfer maternal and fetal information with sources, timing, outstanding tasks, and accepted responsibility.
Language guidance: Patient-reported contraction strength is not a fetal-monitoring interpretation. Use verified sources and timestamps for clinical findings.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I am handing over Samira on the labor ward. / Samira reports stronger contractions. / She asks for an explanation before each examination. / Let us check the current observations and their times. / The monitoring interpretation needs the actual reviewed trace. / This task remains outstanding. / Please confirm who is taking responsibility for that step. / I acknowledge the handoff and the unresolved items. / Please stop me if I have misunderstood. / In everyday language, that means the following. / That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding. / Let me explain that another way. / We can pause before moving to the next point. / Let us confirm who will follow up. / I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed. / We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
Vocabulary: labor progress: Change in the clinical course of labor over time. / contraction pattern: Frequency, duration, and character of uterine contractions in context. / cervical dilation: Opening of the cervix assessed during the relevant examination. / cervical effacement: Thinning of the cervix in the relevant clinical assessment. / fetal station: Position of the presenting part relative to pelvic landmarks. / membrane status: Whether membranes are intact or ruptured as assessed. / rupture time: Reported or established time of membrane rupture. / presenting part: Fetal part positioned toward the birth canal. / fetal heart-rate trace: Recorded fetal heart-rate information for clinical interpretation. / baseline rate: Reference rate assessed over the relevant monitoring interval. / variability: Fluctuation in fetal heart rate assessed within the monitoring context. / deceleration: Decrease in fetal heart rate characterized in clinical context. / uterine activity: Pattern of uterine contractions or related activity. / maternal observations: Verified clinical measurements or findings concerning the patient. / working assessment: Current clinical interpretation subject to relevant review. / outstanding task: Required action not yet completed. / handoff acceptance: Explicit acknowledgment of receiving information and responsibility. / contingency plan: Agreed response to specified possible changes under the actual care plan. / qualified interpreter: Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers. / teach-back: Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning. / patient preference: A person's stated wishes about care or communication. / clinical handoff: Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals. / documented plan: Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved. / source attribution: Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.
Speaking task: Read the corrected handoff. Attribute the patient report separately from verified clinical findings. Swap roles. Confirm responsibility without inventing a tracing category or examination value. Complete the four-line follow-up with the supplied words. Check every explanation, then read the corrected exchange twice, changing roles.
Partner: Dr Chen: Outgoing obstetrician / Dr Ellis: Receiving obstetrician
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A handoff repeats an observation from 10:00 as current at 12:00. The receiving clinician identifies the timestamp error and requests the actual current record.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Dr Chen: That gives us handoff acceptance. Samira's request for explanation remains part of the plan.
Dr Ellis: We also need any actual contingency arrangements explained, not an improvised response to a trace we have not reviewed.
Dr Chen: Agreed. The contingency plan must come from the current clinical assessment and agreed care.
Dr Ellis: I accept the handoff, with the working assessment, sources, unresolved items, and patient preference clearly distinguished.
END REFERENCE
07Taking postpartum concerns seriouslyThe birth date belongs in the urgent handoff+

Your goal

Communicate recent pregnancy context and urgent symptoms without normalizing them as recovery.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

The birth date belongs in the urgent handoff

Twelve days after giving birth, patient Sloane reports shortness of breath and chest pain. The actual emergency response is already active. Dr Patel confirms the recent birth context and symptoms for the receiving team. No diagnosis or measured observations are supplied. This is language practice during an active response, not a guide for deciding whether postpartum symptoms can wait.

Sloane: Postpartum patient / Dr Patel: Obstetrician

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. When did Sloane give birth?

Answer and explanation

B. Twelve days ago

The brief places the birth twelve days before this urgent concern.

2. Which symptoms are reported?

Answer and explanation

B. Shortness of breath and chest pain

Sloane reports breathing difficulty and chest pain without an established cause.

3. What is already active?

Answer and explanation

D. The emergency response

The actual emergency response is active and must not be delayed.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Shortness of breath and chest pain after birth require the actual urgent response. The terminology bank lists possible clinical concepts, not diagnoses established for Sloane.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
postpartum period
Time after childbirth considered in the relevant care context.identify the postpartum period
recent birth history
Relevant information about a birth that occurred recently.communicate recent birth history
postpartum warning sign
Symptom or sign after birth requiring prompt attention.report a postpartum warning sign
shortness of breath
Subjective difficulty or discomfort with breathing.describe shortness of breath
chest pain
Pain or discomfort in the chest requiring contextual assessment.report chest pain
thromboembolism
Obstruction caused by a blood clot traveling through circulation.assess suspected thromboembolism
postpartum cardiomyopathy
Heart-muscle disorder arising around childbirth in the relevant clinical context.evaluate postpartum cardiomyopathy
postpartum hemorrhage
Excessive bleeding after birth under the relevant clinical criteria.assess postpartum hemorrhage
postpartum hypertension
High blood pressure occurring after birth in context.evaluate postpartum hypertension
perinatal mental health
Mental health during pregnancy and the period around childbirth.discuss perinatal mental health
intrusive thought
Unwanted thought experienced as distressing or inconsistent with wishes.clarify an intrusive thought
suicidal thought
Thought involving ending one's own life.ask directly about suicidal thoughts
functional change
Difference in ability to carry out ordinary activities.describe functional change
urgent-care route
Actual service or process for prompt clinical attention.confirm the urgent-care route
postpartum follow-up
Planned review of health and care needs after birth.coordinate postpartum follow-up
handover context
Relevant background accompanying current clinical information.preserve handover context
support network
People and services providing practical or emotional help.identify the support network
continuity of care
Connected care across clinicians, services, and time.maintain continuity of care
qualified interpreter
Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers.request a qualified interpreter
teach-back
Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning.use teach-back without blame
patient preference
A person's stated wishes about care or communication.elicit the patient preference
clinical handoff
Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals.confirm the clinical handoff
documented plan
Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved.review the documented plan
source attribution
Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.preserve source attribution

Mental-health concerns also require appropriate assessment. An intrusive thought and an intention to act are not interchangeable; do not infer safety or risk classification from a glossary definition.

C Notice the language

Recovery is not an explanation for every symptom

Do not dismiss a concern because the patient is tired or recently gave birth. The receiving service needs both current symptoms and relevant recent pregnancy history.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
State recent pregnancy
I gave birth twelve days ago.
Lead with the symptoms
I am reporting shortness of breath and chest pain.
Keep the context
Please include the recent birth in the handoff.
Avoid dismissal
We will not assume this is ordinary recovery.
Preserve diagnostic uncertainty
The cause has not yet been established.
Confirm urgent action
The emergency response is already active.
Separate routine follow-up
A scheduled postpartum visit does not replace urgent assessment.
Close the loop
We will confirm that the receiving team has the relevant history.
Invite correction
Please stop me if I have misunderstood.
Explain a term
In everyday language, that means the following.
Separate uncertainty
That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding.
Check your explanation
Let me explain that another way.
Make space
We can pause before moving to the next point.
Name responsibility
Let us confirm who will follow up.
Keep a record accurate
I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed.
Confirm an agreement
We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
6 language and precision notes
After birth
Adds clinically relevant context without asserting that childbirth caused the symptom.
Not ordinary recovery by assumption
Rejects dismissal while leaving the actual diagnosis to assessment.
Routine versus urgent
Keeps a scheduled follow-up distinct from immediate clinical needs.
Reported versus established
Attribute a person's account with reports or describes. Use confirms only when the relevant evidence supports confirmation.
Can versus will
Can describes an available possibility or ability; will makes a commitment. Do not turn a possible result into a promise.
Checking a message
A check-back confirms the meaning received. Correct the explanation calmly when the listener has understood something different.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which detail belongs near the start of the handoff?

Answer and explanation

B. Birth twelve days ago with current chest pain and breathlessness

The current symptoms and recent birth context are the supplied urgent communication priorities.

2. Which conclusion is unsupported?

Answer and explanation

D. The cause is postpartum cardiomyopathy.

The case supplies no diagnostic evidence establishing a particular cause.

3. What does a routine appointment not replace?

Answer and explanation

A. Actual urgent assessment

A future routine review is not a substitute for the active urgent clinical process.

4. Which mental-health distinction is important?

Answer and explanation

C. An intrusive thought is not automatically an intention to act.

Thought content, distress, intention, and actual risk require appropriate assessment rather than automatic equivalence.

E Conversations

The birth date belongs in the urgent handoff

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Sloane: Postpartum patient / Dr Patel: Obstetrician

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • recent birth history
  • thromboembolism
  • Postpartum cardiomyopathy
  • postpartum warning sign
  • handover context
  • continuity of care
  • shortness of breath
  • chest pain
  • postpartum period
  • Postpartum follow-up
  1. Sloane
    I gave birth twelve days ago, and now I have shortness of breath and chest pain. The emergency response is active.
  2. Dr Patel
    I will make sure the 1 is included with those symptoms as the actual response continues.
  3. Sloane
    I worried that saying I was tired after birth would make people think this was ordinary recovery.
  4. Dr Patel
    A 2 should not be dismissed because you recently gave birth or feel tired.
  5. Sloane
    I can describe how the breathing feels, but I do not know what diagnosis explains it.
  6. Dr Patel
    Describe the 3 in your own words. You do not need to choose a diagnosis before being heard.
  7. Sloane
    The chest discomfort is part of the same concern, and I want it passed on too.
  8. Dr Patel
    I will include the 4 clearly rather than reduce the handoff to a general statement that you feel unwell.
  9. Sloane
    Does mentioning the postpartum period prove that the birth caused whatever is happening?
  10. Dr Patel
    No. The 5 is relevant context, not proof of a particular cause.
  11. Sloane
    I have heard several frightening medical names. Should I tell the team that I definitely have one of them?
  12. Dr Patel
    No. For example, 6 is not a diagnosis you should assign from a list of terms.
  13. Sloane
    And the same is true of the heart condition people sometimes mention around childbirth?
  14. Dr Patel
    Yes. 7 requires the actual clinical evaluation; it has not been established in this conversation.
  15. Sloane
    I have a routine follow-up booked, but I understand that does not replace what is happening now.
  16. Dr Patel
    Correct. 8 and the active urgent response are different parts of care.
  17. Sloane
    Please confirm that the next team knows the timing of the birth, not only the symptoms.
  18. Dr Patel
    We will preserve that 9 and confirm receipt through the actual clinical process.
  19. Sloane
    I want the teams to stay connected afterward as well, rather than assume another service has everything.
  20. Dr Patel
    That is part of 10. The immediate response remains the priority, with appropriate communication between services as care proceeds.

Answers and explanations
  1. recent birth history Turn 2. Recent birth history provides context that belongs alongside the current symptoms.
  2. postpartum warning sign Turn 4. A postpartum warning sign must not be dismissed as ordinary tiredness.
  3. shortness of breath Turn 6. Shortness of breath is a symptom; the patient need not diagnose its cause.
  4. chest pain Turn 8. Chest pain requires assessment and does not identify its cause by itself.
  5. postpartum period Turn 10. The postpartum period identifies the time after childbirth and provides context without independently establishing the cause of a new symptom.
  6. thromboembolism Turn 12. Thromboembolism is a clinical condition requiring appropriate assessment and is not established by appearing in a list of possible concerns.
  7. Postpartum cardiomyopathy Turn 14. Postpartum cardiomyopathy is a heart-muscle disorder requiring clinical evaluation and is not confirmed by the reported symptoms alone.
  8. Postpartum follow-up Turn 16. Postpartum follow-up is planned care after birth and does not replace urgent assessment of a new concerning symptom.
  9. handover context Turn 18. Handover context includes relevant background such as recent birth and must accompany the current symptom report to the receiving team.
  10. continuity of care Turn 20. Continuity of care connects information and responsibility across services and time, including after an urgent episode.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Sloane: I gave birth twelve days ago, and now I have shortness of breath and chest pain. The emergency response is active.
  2. Dr Patel: I will make sure the recent birth history is included with those symptoms as the actual response continues.
  3. Sloane: I worried that saying I was tired after birth would make people think this was ordinary recovery.
  4. Dr Patel: A postpartum warning sign should not be dismissed because you recently gave birth or feel tired.
  5. Sloane: I can describe how the breathing feels, but I do not know what diagnosis explains it.
  6. Dr Patel: Describe the shortness of breath in your own words. You do not need to choose a diagnosis before being heard.
  7. Sloane: The chest discomfort is part of the same concern, and I want it passed on too.
  8. Dr Patel: I will include the chest pain clearly rather than reduce the handoff to a general statement that you feel unwell.
  9. Sloane: Does mentioning the postpartum period prove that the birth caused whatever is happening?
  10. Dr Patel: No. The postpartum period is relevant context, not proof of a particular cause.
  11. Sloane: I have heard several frightening medical names. Should I tell the team that I definitely have one of them?
  12. Dr Patel: No. For example, thromboembolism is not a diagnosis you should assign from a list of terms.
  13. Sloane: And the same is true of the heart condition people sometimes mention around childbirth?
  14. Dr Patel: Yes. Postpartum cardiomyopathy requires the actual clinical evaluation; it has not been established in this conversation.
  15. Sloane: I have a routine follow-up booked, but I understand that does not replace what is happening now.
  16. Dr Patel: Correct. Postpartum follow-up and the active urgent response are different parts of care.
  17. Sloane: Please confirm that the next team knows the timing of the birth, not only the symptoms.
  18. Dr Patel: We will preserve that handover context and confirm receipt through the actual clinical process.
  19. Sloane: I want the teams to stay connected afterward as well, rather than assume another service has everything.
  20. Dr Patel: That is part of continuity of care. The immediate response remains the priority, with appropriate communication between services as care proceeds.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Recent birth belongs at the beginning

A clinician receives a postpartum concern during active emergency care.

  1. Patient: I gave birth twelve days ago and now have chest pain and shortness of breath.
  2. Obstetrician: The actual emergency response is active, and that recent birth history belongs in the handoff.
  3. Patient: I was afraid the symptoms would be dismissed as ordinary tiredness after delivery.
  4. Obstetrician: They should not be normalized by assumption; the actual clinical assessment is necessary.
  5. Patient: I do not know whether the birth caused the symptoms.
  6. Obstetrician: You do not need to establish a cause before reporting the concern.
  7. Patient: Please keep the current symptoms and timing clear when the next team receives the information.
  8. Obstetrician: We will confirm the receiving service and pass on the relevant account.
  9. Patient: This conversation should not delay the response while I search for every detail.
  10. Obstetrician: Correct. Accurate communication supports the active care rather than postponing it.
A routine visit does not replace urgent assessment

A patient distinguishes two care routes.

  1. Patient: I already have a postpartum appointment booked, so I wondered whether every new concern should wait until then.
  2. Obstetrician: A routine appointment is not a substitute for the actual urgent-care instructions.
  3. Patient: I need the real contact routes explained rather than a general promise that recovery is always uncomfortable.
  4. Obstetrician: The service should distinguish routine follow-up from concerns requiring prompt clinical attention.
  5. Patient: Does sending a portal message guarantee that someone has assessed it immediately?
  6. Obstetrician: No. The actual review arrangements and limits need to be understood.
  7. Patient: I should not infer that a message was clinically reviewed simply because it was delivered.
  8. Obstetrician: Correct. Receipt and assessment are different stages.
  9. Patient: Could you check that I understand the actual written plan and its contact details?
  10. Obstetrician: Yes. We should verify the explanation without using this conversation to invent a safe waiting interval.
A mental-health word needs careful clarification

A clinician clarifies a postpartum patient's description.

  1. Patient: I used the word intrusive because the thought is unwanted and distressing, but I need help explaining it.
  2. Obstetrician: We should hear the actual experience and carry out the appropriate assessment.
  3. Patient: I worry that one word will automatically be treated as proof of an intention to act.
  4. Obstetrician: An unwanted thought and an intention are not interchangeable, but neither should be assessed from a glossary alone.
  5. Patient: I want clear questions rather than either immediate dismissal or a conclusion from the label.
  6. Obstetrician: That is appropriate, including the actual safety questions and support process.
  7. Patient: Could you explain the relevant privacy limits before I share more detail?
  8. Obstetrician: We should explain the actual confidentiality and safety duties in this setting.
  9. Patient: Then I can describe the experience without being asked to diagnose myself.
  10. Obstetrician: Exactly. Precise language supports the clinical assessment; it does not determine risk by itself.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

The birth date belongs in the urgent handoff
  1. Read the corrected exchange. Put the birth timing and current symptoms at the start.
  2. Swap roles. Distinguish urgent response from routine follow-up without naming a confirmed cause.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Recent pregnancy was missing from the summary

An urgent handoff lists current symptoms but omits that the patient gave birth twelve days ago. The receiving team acknowledges the added history. The response remains active.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

Answer and explanation

B. twelve

The scenario supplies a birth twelve days before the current concern.

2. Receiver: "I acknowledge the added ___."

Answer and explanation

D. history

The receiving team confirms receipt of the recent birth information.

3. Sender: "The response remains ___."

Answer and explanation

B. active

Adding the missing context does not interrupt the ongoing response.

4. Receiver: "The cause is not yet ___."

Answer and explanation

D. established

The added birth history does not independently establish a diagnosis.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. REFERENCE
Course: Obstetrics English
Professional audience: obstetricians, obstetrics residents, maternal-fetal medicine physicians, maternity-care physicians
Scope: All people, records, figures, and encounters are fictional. This is professional English practice, not medical advice, clinical training, a treatment protocol, or certification. Use current evidence, authorized supervision where required, local law, and your actual clinical and emergency procedures. Do not use an exercise to decide that symptoms can safely wait, select treatment, or calculate a dose. Communication about privacy, consent, and professional duties depends on jurisdiction and setting. Use qualified language support and protect real patient information.
Lesson 07: Taking postpartum concerns seriously
Original fictional case: Twelve days after giving birth, patient Sloane reports shortness of breath and chest pain. The actual emergency response is already active. Dr Patel confirms the recent birth context and symptoms for the receiving team. No diagnosis or measured observations are supplied. This is language practice during an active response, not a guide for deciding whether postpartum symptoms can wait.
Language workshop: The birth date belongs in the urgent handoff
Communication goal: Communicate recent pregnancy context and urgent symptoms without normalizing them as recovery.
Language guidance: Shortness of breath and chest pain after birth require the actual urgent response. The terminology bank lists possible clinical concepts, not diagnoses established for Sloane.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I gave birth twelve days ago. / I am reporting shortness of breath and chest pain. / Please include the recent birth in the handoff. / We will not assume this is ordinary recovery. / The cause has not yet been established. / The emergency response is already active. / A scheduled postpartum visit does not replace urgent assessment. / We will confirm that the receiving team has the relevant history. / Please stop me if I have misunderstood. / In everyday language, that means the following. / That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding. / Let me explain that another way. / We can pause before moving to the next point. / Let us confirm who will follow up. / I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed. / We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
Vocabulary: postpartum period: Time after childbirth considered in the relevant care context. / recent birth history: Relevant information about a birth that occurred recently. / postpartum warning sign: Symptom or sign after birth requiring prompt attention. / shortness of breath: Subjective difficulty or discomfort with breathing. / chest pain: Pain or discomfort in the chest requiring contextual assessment. / thromboembolism: Obstruction caused by a blood clot traveling through circulation. / postpartum cardiomyopathy: Heart-muscle disorder arising around childbirth in the relevant clinical context. / postpartum hemorrhage: Excessive bleeding after birth under the relevant clinical criteria. / postpartum hypertension: High blood pressure occurring after birth in context. / perinatal mental health: Mental health during pregnancy and the period around childbirth. / intrusive thought: Unwanted thought experienced as distressing or inconsistent with wishes. / suicidal thought: Thought involving ending one's own life. / functional change: Difference in ability to carry out ordinary activities. / urgent-care route: Actual service or process for prompt clinical attention. / postpartum follow-up: Planned review of health and care needs after birth. / handover context: Relevant background accompanying current clinical information. / support network: People and services providing practical or emotional help. / continuity of care: Connected care across clinicians, services, and time. / qualified interpreter: Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers. / teach-back: Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning. / patient preference: A person's stated wishes about care or communication. / clinical handoff: Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals. / documented plan: Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved. / source attribution: Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.
Speaking task: Read the corrected exchange. Put the birth timing and current symptoms at the start. Swap roles. Distinguish urgent response from routine follow-up without naming a confirmed cause. Complete the four-line follow-up with the supplied words. Check every explanation, then read the corrected exchange twice, changing roles.
Partner: Sloane: Postpartum patient / Dr Patel: Obstetrician
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: An urgent handoff lists current symptoms but omits that the patient gave birth twelve days ago. The receiving team acknowledges the added history. The response remains active.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Sloane: Please confirm that the next team knows the timing of the birth, not only the symptoms.
Dr Patel: We will preserve that handover context and confirm receipt through the actual clinical process.
Sloane: I want the teams to stay connected afterward as well, rather than assume another service has everything.
Dr Patel: That is part of continuity of care. The immediate response remains the priority, with appropriate communication between services as care proceeds.
END REFERENCE

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

08Discussing confirmed pregnancy lossClear words and space to respond+

Your goal

Give difficult news directly and compassionately, then explain an individualized options discussion.

This lesson in the book, page 67 ↗

A Read the situation

Clear words and space to respond

After the appropriate clinical assessment has confirmed an early pregnancy loss, Dr Gray meets patient Eden. Eden asks whether the finding is certain and worries that an ordinary daily activity caused the loss. The cause has not been established. Dr Gray explains the confirmed finding, allows silence, asks about chosen support, and arranges discussion of the actual management options. No procedure, medicine, or follow-up interval is prescribed.

Dr Gray: Obstetrician / Eden: Patient

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What has been confirmed?

Answer and explanation

B. An early pregnancy loss after appropriate assessment

The finding is confirmed, while cause and management choice remain separate questions.

2. What worries Eden?

Answer and explanation

C. That an ordinary activity caused the loss

Eden expresses self-blame about an ordinary daily activity.

3. What is not prescribed?

Answer and explanation

C. A management option or follow-up interval

The case arranges an individualized discussion rather than prescribing treatment or timing.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Here the loss is confirmed; its cause is not. Do not weaken a confirmed finding with misleading ambiguity or invent a cause to answer self-blame.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
early pregnancy loss
Loss of a pregnancy early in gestation under the relevant definition.explain early pregnancy loss
miscarriage
Common term for spontaneous pregnancy loss in the relevant clinical context.discuss a miscarriage
confirmed finding
Conclusion established through the appropriate assessment.explain a confirmed finding
diagnostic uncertainty
Unresolved question about whether a diagnosis is established.distinguish diagnostic uncertainty
viability assessment
Clinical assessment of whether a pregnancy is continuing as expected.review the viability assessment
expectant management
Management involving observation under an appropriate clinical plan.discuss expectant management
medical management
Management using prescribed medicines under the actual care plan.explain medical management
surgical management
Management involving an appropriate procedure.discuss surgical management
management preference
Person's informed preference among relevant care options.elicit a management preference
retained pregnancy tissue
Pregnancy-related tissue remaining in the uterus in context.assess retained pregnancy tissue
bleeding history
Account of bleeding timing, amount, and associated concerns.obtain a bleeding history
follow-up assessment
Subsequent review of clinical status and care needs.arrange follow-up assessment
bereavement support
Support offered after a significant loss.offer bereavement support
self-blame
Belief that one is personally responsible for an adverse event.respond to self-blame
empathetic silence
Deliberate space allowing a person to respond to difficult news.allow empathetic silence
acknowledgment of loss
Direct recognition of the significance of the person's loss.offer acknowledgment of loss
support preference
Person's wishes about who or what will support them.ask about support preferences
care summary
Clear record of the finding, discussion, and agreed next steps.provide a care summary
qualified interpreter
Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers.request a qualified interpreter
teach-back
Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning.use teach-back without blame
patient preference
A person's stated wishes about care or communication.elicit the patient preference
clinical handoff
Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals.confirm the clinical handoff
documented plan
Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved.review the documented plan
source attribution
Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.preserve source attribution

Management depends on the actual clinical situation and informed discussion. The vocabulary names options without supplying a regimen, procedure choice, or safe waiting period.

C Notice the language

Do not rush toward reassurance

Avoid minimizing phrases such as at least it was early or you can try again. State the confirmed finding clearly, allow a response, and ask what support the person wants.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Give clear news
I am sorry. The assessment has confirmed that the pregnancy has ended.
Allow space
We can pause here.
Separate certainty
The finding is confirmed; the cause has not been established.
Respond to blame
Nothing in this assessment establishes that your ordinary activity caused this.
Avoid minimization
I recognize that this is a significant loss for you.
Ask about support
Who would you like with you for the next discussion?
Introduce options gently
When you are ready, I will explain the options relevant to your situation.
Close concretely
We will provide the agreed care plan and the appropriate contact route.
Invite correction
Please stop me if I have misunderstood.
Explain a term
In everyday language, that means the following.
Separate uncertainty
That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding.
Check your explanation
Let me explain that another way.
Make space
We can pause before moving to the next point.
Name responsibility
Let us confirm who will follow up.
Keep a record accurate
I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed.
Confirm an agreement
We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
6 language and precision notes
Confirmed versus suspected
Matches the certainty of the actual finding instead of using vague words to soften difficult news.
Cause versus finding
Explains that certainty about what happened does not establish why it happened.
When you are ready
Makes space within the actual clinical circumstances without promising unlimited delay or prescribing timing.
Reported versus established
Attribute a person's account with reports or describes. Use confirms only when the relevant evidence supports confirmation.
Can versus will
Can describes an available possibility or ability; will makes a commitment. Do not turn a possible result into a promise.
Checking a message
A check-back confirms the meaning received. Correct the explanation calmly when the listener has understood something different.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which statement matches the case?

Answer and explanation

D. The loss is confirmed, but the cause is not established.

The case separates the confirmed clinical finding from the unresolved cause.

2. Which phrase minimizes the loss?

Answer and explanation

B. At least it was early.

At least it was early imposes a minimizing comparison rather than acknowledging the person's experience.

3. What does empathetic silence allow?

Answer and explanation

B. Space for the person to respond

A pause allows a response without rushing the person toward reassurance or a decision.

4. Which action is not supplied?

Answer and explanation

D. Selecting a management regimen

The scenario supplies no treatment selection, medicine regimen, or clinical follow-up interval.

E Conversations

Clear words and space to respond

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Dr Gray: Obstetrician / Eden: Patient

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

  • management preference
